Deformed GOE


Abstract




Almost-optimal bulk regularity conditions in the CLT for Wigner matrices

Benjamin Landon

Philippe Sosoe

University of Toronto

Cornell University

Department of Mathematics

Department of Mathematics

blandon@math.toronto.edu

ps934@cornell.edu

August 7, 2026

Abstract:
We consider linear spectral statistics of the form
\mathrm{tr}(\varphi(H))
for test functions
\varphi
of low regularity and Wigner matrices
H
with smooth entry distribution. We show that for functions
\varphi
in the Sobolev space
H^{1/2+\varepsilon}
or the space
C^{1/2+\varepsilon}
, that are supported within the spectral bulk of the semicircle distribution, these linear spectral statistics have asymptotic Gaussian fluctuations with the same variance as in the CLT for functions of higher regularity, for any
\varepsilon>0
.

1
Introduction

This work is concerned with the fluctuations of linear spectral statistics of Wigner matrices
H
for low regularity functions. Denote the eigenvalues of
H
in increasing order by

\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{N}.

Wigner’s semicircle law states that for smooth
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

(1.1)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi(\lambda_{i})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(x)\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x,

almost surely,
where

(1.2)

\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{(4-x^{2})_{+}}.

We will be interested in the asymptotic fluctuations of these linear spectral statistics.
The centered linear spectral statistic associated to
\varphi
and
H
is,

(1.3)

\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi):=\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H)-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H)].

Generically, one expects that
\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi)
converges as
N\to\infty
in distribution to a Gaussian random variable with variance

\displaystyle V_{H}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{-2}^{2}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}\frac{4-xy}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\sqrt{4-y^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(1.4)

\displaystyle+\frac{s_{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(\int_{-2}^{2}\varphi(x)\frac{2-x^{2}}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{2},

where
s_{4}
is the fourth cumulant of the matrix entries, which is
0
in the case of the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble (GOE). Early works on the fluctuations of linear spectral statistics include the work of Arkharov
[
2
]
who announced the convergence of normalized traces of sample covariance matrices to Gaussian random variables, the work of Jonsson
[
41
]
who gave a proof of this result, and Girko
[
32
]
who studied the fluctuations of the traces of resolvents of Wigner and sample covariance matrices. Since these works there has emerged a large literature proving central limit theorems for various classes of test functions and classes of random matrices. We refer the interested reader to, e.g., the works
[
47
,
37
,
35
,
16
,
46
,
1
,
4
,
15
,
18
]
and the references therein.

A common feature of all these results is that the limiting variance contains a term whose most singular part is a double integral of

(1.5)

\frac{(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}.

This extends to models even beyond Wigner or covariance matrices, such as beta ensembles
[
40
,
6
]
, discrete beta ensembles
[
8
]
, as well as various determinantal point processes whose kernels are given by or approximate a spectral projection
[
54
,
53
,
21
]
.

Of particular interest is the extent to which these results depend on the regularity of
\varphi
. Johansson
[
40
]
obtained the central limit theorem for a class of unitarily invariant Hermitian matrices and general
\varphi\in H^{2+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R})
. Here, we recall that the homogeneous and inhomogeneous Sobolev spaces,
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})=\dot{H}^{s}
and
H^{s}=H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
are the spaces of all functions such that the norms

(1.6)

\|\varphi\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{s}}:=\int|\hat{\varphi}(\xi)|^{2}|\xi|^{2s}\mathrm{d}\xi,\qquad\|\varphi\|^{2}_{H^{s}}:=\int|\hat{\varphi}(\xi)|^{2}(1+|\xi|^{2s})\mathrm{d}\xi

are finite,
where
\hat{\varphi}
denotes the Fourier transform. The significance of the expression (
1.5
) in the context of these Sobolev spaces is that,

(1.7)

\|\varphi\|_{\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}=c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

for a numerical constant
c>0
.

Johansson further conjectured that the sole condition under which the CLT holds should be that the limiting expression for the variance is finite. Given the similarity of (
1.7
) with the most singular component of (
1
), it is natural to investigate this conjecture via test functions in the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
, the goal being to get as close to
s=1/2
as possible. The Hölder spaces
C^{s}=C^{s}(\mathbb{R})
are also natural function spaces suited to this purpose.

Shcherbina
[
52
]
obtained the CLT for the functions in the space
H^{3/2+\varepsilon}
, and Lytova and Pastur obtained a CLT for the GOE and
\varphi\in C^{1}

[
47
]
. The second author with Wong
[
55
]
obtained various results for complex Hermitian Wigner matrices, including a CLT for the class
C^{1/2+\varepsilon}
under moment conditions, as well as an unconditional result for the space
H^{1+\varepsilon}
, the latter being the sharpest available for general Wigner matrices. The sharpest result in terms of regularity is the result of Kopel who showed that in the case of the GUE, the CLT holds if and only if the expression for the limiting variance (
1
) is finite
[
42
]
.

On the other hand, consider
\varphi(x)=\bm{1}_{\{x\leq E\}}
for
E\in(-2,2)
. For the GUE, Gustavsson showed that the linear statistic
\mathcal{N}_{N}[\varphi]
quantity has Gaussian fluctuations with variance of order
\log(N)

[
36
]
. This result was extended to the GOE by O’Rourke as well as to Wigner matrices under the additional condition that the first four moments match those of the Gaussian ensemble
[
50
]
. This was also studied by Dallaporta and Vu
[
19
]
. These results were extended to general Wigner matrices by the authors
[
44
]
and independently and in parallel by Bourgade and Mody
[
13
]
. Note that for the indicator function of a compact interval, the expression (
1
) is infinite, but in terms of Fourier modes, the divergence is logarithmic:

\int_{|\xi|\leq N}|\xi||\widehat{1}_{I}(\xi)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\sim\log N.

For general Wigner matrices, a result as sharp as that of Kopel
[
42
]
seems beyond the reach of current methods. However, the aforementioned results
[
44
,
13
]
on the indicator function give some evidence of the necessity of the limiting expression
V_{H}(\varphi)
being finite. In the present work we will show that, at least in the spectral bulk, finiteness of
V_{H}(\varphi)
is almost sufficient, for a general class of Wigner matrices with smooth distribution.

The question of the dependence of the fluctuations on the regularity of the function
\varphi
has an essentially complete answer for linear statistics of the
Circular
Unitary Ensemble (CUE) on
\mathbb{T}=[0,2\pi)
, whose joint density is proportional to

(1.8)

\prod_{j<k}|e^{i\theta_{j}}-e^{i\theta_{k}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{\theta}.

The normalized density (
1.8
) is that of the eigenvalues of a unitary random matrix chosen from the Haar measure.

For the purposes of discussing the CUE, we let, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(1.9)

\widehat{f}(k)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\mathrm{e}^{-ik\theta}f(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})\,\mathrm{d}\theta

denote the
k
th Fourier coefficient of the function
f
on the torus (we will not consider such functions outside of the introduction).
Diaconis and Evans
[
22
]
, building on previous work by Diaconis and Shashahani
[
23
]
, showed that the quantity

(1.10)

\mathcal{N}_{f,\mathrm{CUE}}:=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N}f(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{j}})-\frac{N}{2\pi}\int f(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})\,\mathrm{d}\theta}{\sqrt{\sum_{-N}^{N}|k||\widehat{f}(k)|^{2}}}

converges in distribution to normal random variable with mean zero and variance 1, provided the sequence
c_{n}=\sum_{-n}^{n}k^{2}|\widehat{f}(k)|^{2}
is regularly varying. In particular, if
f
belongs to the Sobolev
\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
of functions for which

(1.11)

\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}:=\sum_{-\infty}^{\infty}|k||\widehat{f}(k)|^{2}<\infty,

then
\sum_{j=1}^{N}f(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}})-\frac{N}{2\pi}\int f(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})\,\mathrm{d}\theta
converges to a normally distributed random variable whose variance coincides with the
\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})
norm of
f
.
We note that the
\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{T})
also has a physical space expression analogous to the first term in (
1
)

(1.12)

\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1/2}}=c\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{(f(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})-f(\mathrm{e}^{i\nu}))^{2}}{\sin\left(\frac{\theta-\nu}{2}\right)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\nu.

Diaconis and Evans’ result also subsumes both the CLT for smooth functions
f
, and the analog for the CUE of the result concerning indicator functions of intervals. When
f
is such an indicator of
I\subset\mathbb{T}
, the sum
\sum_{-N}^{N}k^{2}|\widehat{f}(k)|^{2}
diverges logarithmically.

As stated above, the optimal condition for convergence obtained by Kopel for the GUE is more complicated than being in
\dot{H}^{1/2}
, but if the support of
\varphi
is compactly contained in
(-2,2)
, then the main (universal) term in (
1
) is bounded above and below by the expression,

(1.13)

\int_{-2}^{2}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Under the above support condition, the integral form of the
\dot{H}^{1/2}
norm (
1.7
) is a norm-equivalent to the expression (
1.13
), as well as the inhomogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1/2}}
. We therefore turn to whether the CLT holds for functions in the space
H^{1/2+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R})
.

The significance of the
H^{1/2}
threshold can also be understood by looking at the special case where

(1.14)

\varphi(x)=\varphi_{E,\eta}(x):=\frac{1}{x-E-\mathrm{i}\eta},

with
N
-dependent
\eta
.
In this case,
\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi)
is the trace of the resolvent matrix, and the
local semicircle law
(see (
2.11
) below), implies that, in a very strong sense, the fluctuations of
\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi_{E,\eta})
are of order
O(1/\eta)
, up to an
N^{\varepsilon}
error:

(1.15)

\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi_{E,\eta})-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi_{E,\eta})]=\mathcal{O}(N^{\varepsilon}/\eta)

with very high probability. This is the order of fluctuations predicted by the
H^{1/2}
norm:

(1.16)

\|\varphi_{E,\eta}\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}\approx\|\partial_{x}\varphi_{E,\eta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\varphi_{E,\eta}\|_{L^{2}}\approx\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}.

In contrast, general purpose concentration tools such as log-Sobolev inequalities do not capture the correct order of fluctuations in the local regime for Wigner matrices because the bounds they provide are expressed in terms of the Lipschitz norms of the functions.

Our main result, Theorem
1.4
below, is that for any
\varepsilon>0
there is a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
so that,

(1.17)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi))\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2+\varepsilon}}^{2}

for
\varphi
with support contained in
[-2+\varepsilon,2-\varepsilon]
and
H
a Wigner matrix whose entries have a smooth distribution (the precise definition appears in the next section). The CLT for such
\varphi
follows by approximation by smooth functions and the existing literature on linear spectral statistics.

Up to the
\varepsilon>0
factor, this is expected to be optimal as the CLT as stated does not hold for functions in
H^{1/2-\varepsilon}
as shown by the example of the indicator function.
1
†
†
footnotetext:
1
. Given the results of Diaconis and Evans
[
22
]
, it is natural to conjecture is that, given any function
\varphi
, a CLT holds after renormalizing
\varphi
by the
H^{1/2}
norm of its projection onto its first
N
Fourier modes. While it may be possible to prove such a result for the Gaussian cases, this seems out of reach of current techniques for general ensembles.
These are the strongest results available for general Wigner matrices. In fact, before our work, the CLT was not known for even the GOE case for functions
\varphi
not admitting a classical derivative.

1.1
Statement of main results

We first define the matrix ensembles we investigate in the current work.

Definition 1.1

(Real symmetric Wigner matrices)
.

A real symmetric
Wigner matrix

H
is an
N\times N
symmetric matrix such that the upper triangular part
\{H_{ij}\}_{i\leq j}
are independent centered random variables such that,

(1.18)

\mathbb{E}[H_{ij}^{2}]=\frac{1+\delta_{ij}}{N}.

We furthermore assume that the random variables
\{H_{ij}\}_{1\leq i<j\leq N}
are iid and that the random variables
\{H_{ii}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
are iid.
We furthermore assume that cumulants obey,

(1.19)

\kappa_{k}(H_{11})=2^{1-k}\kappa_{k}(H_{12})

for
0\leq k\leq 4
. Additionally, for any
k>0
we assume that there is a
C_{k}>0
so that,

(1.20)

\mathbb{E}[|H_{ij}|^{k}]\leq\frac{C_{k}}{N^{k/2}}.

The assumption (
1.19
) is mainly for notational convenience in some resolvent expansions, and could be removed with further effort. Note that if
X
is a (non-symmetric) matrix of iid centered random variables of variance
1/N
then (
1.18
) and (
1.19
) is satisfied by
H=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(X+X^{T})
.

Due to use of Wegner estimates as well as the reverse heat flow, we will require the densities of the matrix elements to have some smoothness. We formulate this in the following.

Definition 1.2

(Smooth densities)
.

We say a probability density
h(x)
is Wigner-smooth if there is a
\delta>0
so that

(1.21)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left[\delta|x|^{2}\right]h(x)\mathrm{d}x<\infty

and for every
k
there is a
C>0
so that,

(1.22)

\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}^{k}x}\log h(x)\right|\leq C(1+|x|)^{C}.

Definition 1.3

(Smooth Wigner matrices)
.

We say that a real symmetric Wigner matrix is smooth if there are two Wigner-smooth probability densities
h_{o}(x)
and
h_{d}(x)
such that the off-diagonal entries are distributed according to
h_{o}
and the diagonal entries are distributed according to
h_{d}
.

The smoothness conditions above could be relaxed somewhat. For example, the sub-Gaussian tails (
1.21
) assumption could be significantly relaxed. We include it so that we can directly cite the Wegner estimates of
[
24
]
. However, as noted in
[
10
]
, the relevant estimates hold under weaker conditions. For simplicity we just assume (
1.21
). The smoothness conditions (
1.22
) could also be relaxed, as in
[
9
]
, in that the constants on the RHS could be assumed to diverge like
N^{c_{1}k}
for some
c_{1}>0
. The
c_{1}>0
would then taken to be small depending on the parameter
\varepsilon>0
in the assumption that
\varphi\in H^{1/2+\varepsilon}
. Again, as our arguments are quite long we do not pursue this generalization.

Results for discrete ensembles and
\varphi
in the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
seem out of reach of current methods due to the use of Wegner estimates to cut-off very high-frequency modes. However, some results for discrete ensembles and
\varphi\in C^{s}
for various
1/2<s<1
can be proven. This requires further arguments and will be discussed in future work.

The main result of the paper is the following variance bound.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
H
be a smooth real symmetric Wigner matrix as in Definition
1.3
. For any
\varepsilon>0
and
\kappa>0
, there is a
C>0
so that the following holds. For any
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
supported in
(-2+\kappa,2-\kappa)
we have,

(1.23)

\mathrm{Var}\left(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi(H))\right)\leq C_{\varepsilon}\|\varphi\|^{2}_{H^{1/2+\varepsilon}}.

By approximating
\varphi
by smooth functions one easily obtains the following.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
H
be a smooth real symmetric Wigner matrix and
\varphi
as in Theorem
1.4
. Then the centered linear spectral statistic
\mathcal{N}_{N}(\varphi)
converges to a Gaussian random variable with variance given by (
1
).

Note that under our support conditions, it follows from the integral representation of the
\dot{H}^{s}
norm that

(1.24)

\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2+\varepsilon}}\leq C_{\delta}\|\varphi\|_{C^{1/2+\varepsilon+\delta}}

and so the above results hold also for
\varphi\in C^{1/2+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Our methods also allow one to calculate the usual corrections to the expectation (i.e., the next order terms after
\int\varphi(x)\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x
) for
\varphi\in H^{1/2+\varepsilon}
. We summarize this in the following remark.

Remark 1.6
.

Let
H
and
\varphi
be as above. The methods of our paper can be used to derive the expansion,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H)]-N\int\varphi(x)\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\varphi(x)\frac{1}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x

(1.25)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{s_{4}}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}\varphi(x)\frac{x^{4}-4x^{2}+2}{\sqrt{4-x}}\mathrm{d}x+o(1)

where
s_{4}
is the fourth cumulant of the off-diagonal entries.

This is technically easier than the estimate on the variance and so we do not provide the details. Note that usually there is an additional term on the RHS involving
\varphi(\pm 2)
. However, by the compact support condition of
\varphi
this term is absent.

Finally, we also remark that the condition that
\varphi
vanishes near the spectral edges is not strictly necessary under the condition that
\varphi
is smooth near the edges. For example, one could obtain a CLT if
\varphi=\varphi_{1}+\varphi_{2}
where
\varphi_{1}
is as in the above results and
\varphi_{2}
is sufficiently smooth, but is allowed to be non-zero near the edges
\pm 2
.

1.2
Methodology

As our work is rather involved, we now summarize our methods and give a detailed proof sketch.

The starting point of our methods is a modification of the Littlewood-Paley decomposition introduced in the work of the second author with Wong in
[
55
]
. Roughly, this decomposes
\varphi
into the sum,

(1.26)

\varphi=\sum_{k\geq-1}\varphi_{k}=\sum_{k}(P_{2^{-k}})\star g_{k}

where
P_{\eta}
denotes the Poisson kernel

(1.27)

P_{\eta}(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\eta}{x^{2}+\eta^{2}}

and
\varphi_{k}
and
g_{k}
whose Fourier support is contained in frequencies only of order
2^{k}
. The quantities,

(1.28)

\sum_{k}2^{2ks}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\sum_{k}2^{2ks}\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}

are then comparable to
H^{s}
norm of
\varphi
. The main advantage of the last representation on the right side of (
1.26
) is that when taking the trace against
H
, this becomes a convolution against

(1.29)

\mathrm{tr}\,P_{\eta}(H-E)=\frac{1}{\pi}\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{tr}\frac{1}{H-z},\qquad z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta,

i.e., a function of the resolvent
G(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
. The resolvent is highly studied in random matrix theory (see for example
[
7
]
and
[
28
, Chapter 6]
) and there are many techniques available to estimate it.

The decomposition (
1.26
) further allows us to deal with different ranges of frequencies with different methods. In particular, the behavior of
\varphi
near the frequency
2^{k}
involves only the behaviour of the eigenvalues on the scale
\eta:=2^{-k}
. We now discuss how the various frequency ranges are handled.

Due to the embedding of
H^{1/2+\mathfrak{a}}
into
L^{\infty}
for any
\mathfrak{a}>0
and the trivial estimate
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}f(H))\leq N^{2}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}
, the frequencies larger than
2^{k}\geq N^{C}
for some large
C>0
depending on
\mathfrak{a}>0
are easily dispensed with. Moreover, the projection of
\varphi
onto frequencies
2^{k}\leq N^{c}
for small
c>0
yields a function whose
C^{5}
norm grows less than
N^{c^{\prime}}
for small
c^{\prime}>0
depending on
c>0
. Such a function is sufficiently smooth so that it can be handled by the existing resolvent techniques for proving CLTs in random matrix theory.

This leaves estimating the variance of the logarithmically many
\varphi_{k}
for
N^{c}\leq 2^{k}\leq N^{C}
. By Cauchy-Schwarz, it suffices to prove the estimate

(1.30)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))\leq CN^{\varepsilon}2^{k}\left(\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

for any
\varepsilon>0
and
for
k
in this range. These estimates are the main technical contribution of the current work.

The first simplification is that, by the reverse heat flow
[
26
]
and our smoothness assumptions, we can assume that
H
is a Gaussian divisible ensemble of the form,

(1.31)

H_{t}:=\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}W+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}}G

for any
t=o(1)
,
W
a smooth Wigner matrix and
G
a matrix from the GOE. This allows us to use Dyson Brownian motion techniques to analyze the eigenvalue behavior. In particular, we will use the approach to DBM using the stochastic advection equation developed in the work of Bourgade
[
9
]
. These results play a fundamental role in our approach as the work
[
9
]
provides essentially optimal estimates which turn out to be crucial; weaker estimates would not suffice.

Here, we split these estimates into three regimes, using different techniques in each range. The three regimes are:

(1)

The mesoscopic and microscopic regimes
N^{c}\leq 2^{k}\leq C_{1}N
,

(2)

The submicroscopic regime
C_{1}N\leq 2^{k}\leq N^{2-\varepsilon_{1}}
,

(3)

The very high frequency regime
N^{2-\varepsilon_{1}}\leq 2^{k}\leq N^{C}
.

Above,
\varepsilon_{1}>0
is chosen sufficiently small depending on the
\mathfrak{a}>0
such that
\varphi\in H^{1/2+\mathfrak{a}}
.
The tightest threshold is for frequences
2^{k}\approx C_{1}N
: the techniques we use in the regime
(1)
work up to
k
satisfying
2^{k}\leq C_{1}N
for any
C_{1}>0
but will not work for
2^{k}\approx N^{1+\varepsilon}
. On the other hand, the techniques we use in the regime
(2)
work only for
2^{k}\geq C_{1}N
for some sufficiently large
C_{1}>0
.

The regime
(3)
is easiest. The desired variance bound follows by Cauchy-Schwarz and a Wegner estimate
[
24
]
,

(1.32)

\sup_{E\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}[|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|^{-1}]\leq C^{\prime}N^{1+\varepsilon}

for any
\eta>N^{-C}
and
\varepsilon>0
. In fact, this argument does not depend on the Gaussian divisibility of
H_{t}
.

The treatment of the remaining regimes
(1)
and
(2)
relies heavily on the Gaussian divisibility and Dyson Brownian Motion (DBM). One main input is the work of Bourgade
[
9
]
that provides an almost-optimal approach to DBM. Roughly, this work exhibits a coupling between the eigenvalues of
H_{t}
(denoted by
\lambda_{i}
) and the eigenvalues of the GOE (denoted by
\mu_{i}
) such that,

(1.33)

\lambda_{i}=\mu_{i}+N^{-1}(\Phi_{t}(W)-\Phi_{t}(G^{\prime}))+N^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}t}\right),

where
\Phi_{t}(A):=\mathrm{tr}f_{t}(A)
and
f_{t}
is an approximate step function on the scale
t
centered at
\gamma_{i}
,
i
th
N
-quantile of
\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}
,
W
is the smooth Wigner matrix in the definition of
H_{t}
and
G^{\prime}
is an auxilliary GOE matrix. In particular,
W
is independent of
\mu_{i}
and
G^{\prime}
.

If
t
is close to
1
, the estimate (
1.33
) allows one to access the behavior of
\lambda_{i}
down to the scale
N^{-2}
. Given that the reductions outlined above reduce our proof to the regime
2^{k}\leq N^{2-\varepsilon}
it makes sense that Bourgade’s result gives some hope of proving our desired estimates. We now discuss the implementation of these ideas. It turns out to be rather different in the regimes
(1)
and
(2)
, but we first make some general remarks applicable to both cases.

The estimate (
1.33
) says that
\lambda_{i}
is given by a universal random variable,
\mu_{i}-\Phi_{t}(G^{\prime})
up to the quantity
\Phi_{t}(W)
. If one can show that the behavior of
\Phi_{t}(W)
is independent of the entry distribution, or otherwise control it, then one can relate the behavior of
\lambda_{i}
directly to the universal behavior. However, this approach cannot be implemented in such a simple minded fashion. There are many highly non-trivial obstacles:

(i)

The behavior of
\Phi_{t}(W)
is in fact not universal. Asymptotically, it is Gaussian but the variance is non-universal as it depends on
s_{4}
through the formula (
1
)

(ii)

Since we need to control
\lambda_{i}
down to the scale
N^{-2+\varepsilon_{1}}
, we would need to calculate, for example,

(1.34)

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\lambda\Phi_{t}(W)}]

for
\lambda
up to
N^{1-\varepsilon_{1}}
. Resolvent techniques are available to study this quantity, but we expect that such fine estimates would require significant technical effort and numerous iterated expansions. Moreover, one also requires estimates for large
\lambda
with extremely small errors as well.

(iii)

Even if one can show that
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(H_{t}))\approx\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(G))
for
G
from the GOE, this still leaves one the task of calculating the quantity on the RHS. To our knowledge, the required estimates for the GOE are lacking from the literature (estimates for the GUE seem to be available).

(iv)

It is difficult to even get started with the use of (
1.33
) to replace
\lambda_{i}
by the
\mu_{i}
in
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(H_{t}))
with an acceptable error. This in fact occupies a significant component of our work.

Let us discuss how these obstacles are overcome. They appear in different guises in each of the regimes
(1)
and
(2)
. First, recalling
g_{k}
above, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H_{t}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}G(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)],\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}G(y+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(1.35)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1},\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Note that there is a well-known estimate,

(1.36)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}G(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)])\leq N^{\varepsilon}\eta^{-2}

available for any
\varepsilon>0
and large ranges of
\eta>0
. Using this in (
1.2
) would yield
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))\leq N^{\varepsilon}\eta^{-2}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
. This is too lossy, as one needs an estimate with a factor
\eta^{-1}
instead of
\eta^{-2}
.

Regime
(2)
.

We first discuss the submicroscopic regime
2^{k}\gg N
.
In the introduction, we mentioned the conjecture that the CLT should hold for any
\varphi
after renormalizing by the projection
\sum_{k\leq N}\varphi_{k}
of
\varphi
onto its first
N
Fourier modes. We would then hope that the frequences with
2^{k}\gg N
do not contribute to the asymptotic fluctuations. This is supported by the following heuristic.

We will focus on the contribution of
\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(H))^{2}]
to the variance, as this will explain our approach. Consider the representation on the last line of (
1.2
) and apply the estimate (
1.33
) twice, once each for
\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{j}
, yielding two mesoscopic linear statistics of
W
which we denote by
\Xi_{i}
and
\Xi_{j}
. Assuming that the
\lambda_{i}
can be replaced by the expression on the right side at an acceptable error, one would find by Fourier duality,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\mu}_{i}-x-N^{-1}\Xi_{i}-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\mu}_{j}-y-N^{-1}\Xi_{j}-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(1.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\hat{g}_{k}(\xi_{1})\hat{g}_{k}(\xi_{2})\hat{F}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\xi_{1}N^{-1}\Xi_{1}+\xi_{2}N^{-1}\Xi_{2})]}]\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

where

(1.38)

F(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\mu}_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\mu}_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right]

and
\hat{\mu}_{i}
involves only
\mu_{i}
and
G^{\prime}
and is independent of
W
. It turns out that the
\Xi_{i}
are asymptotically Gaussian with variance growing as
\log(N)
and so one expects that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\xi_{i}N^{-1}\Xi_{i}}]\leq\mathrm{e}^{-c\xi^{2}\log(N)N^{-2}}
. For
|\xi_{i}|\geq C_{1}N
for
C_{1}
sufficiently large, this decay is sufficient to completely remove any contribution of the
g_{k}
’s to the overall variance, because the
g_{k}
’s are supported only in frequences of order
2^{k}\geq C_{1}N
in the regime
(2)
.

However, it is difficult to implement this directly. First, in fact the
\Xi_{i}
are highly correlated if
t
is of the same order as
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}
(here, we denote the
N
-quantiles or classical locations of the semicircle distribution by
\gamma_{i}
, which are the typical locations of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
). This will destroy the decay of the joint Fourier transform of
(\Xi_{i},\Xi_{j})
. Therefore, one sees that one must apply (
1.33
) using a time
t_{ij}
adapted to the distance
\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}
, i.e.,
t_{ij}:=N^{-c_{1}}|\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}|
, for some
c_{1}>0
. Note that then if
i
and
j
are quite close,
t_{ij}
is quite short. That the error incurred by taking this
t_{ij}
in the first approximation in (
1.2
) is acceptable, of order
\mathcal{O}(\eta^{-1}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
), seems to be a minor miracle in itself (here, it is required to take
c_{1}>0
adapted to the
\mathfrak{a}>0
in the assumption
\varphi\in H^{1/2+\mathfrak{a}}
). Establishing this requires significant effort and delicate estimates. This is the content of Proposition
4.4
below.

Still, this approach then requires handling the Fourier transform of
\Xi_{i}
for large frequencies, as well as obtaining very precise estimates with a small error term. Since
\Xi_{i}
is a mesoscopic spectral statistic, this could in principle be handled by resolvent expansions. Such an approach may be possible, but it would require significant effort and resolvent expansions to arbitrarily high order. Even for global statistics where
t_{ij}\approx 1
, obtaining optimal estimates would require significant effort. See, e.g.
[
5
]
, where strong estimates are obtained, but would still not be sufficient for our purposes.

Instead, we observe that we require only
decay
of the joint Fourier transform of the
(\Xi_{i},\Xi_{j})
and not a precise asymptotic estimate. We therefore apply Dyson Brownian Motion again, and realize
W
itself as a Gaussian divisible ensemble. The form of
\Xi_{i}
is that of an
\arctan(\cdot)
function, or
\mathrm{Im}[\log(\cdot)]
. It turns out that the SDE obeyed by
\mathrm{tr}\log(H-z)
under DBM is relatively simple and amenable to study using the method of characteristics as in
[
39
]
. This is carried out in Section
7
.

This allows for a decomposition of
\Xi_{i}\approx Z_{i}+A_{i}
where
Z_{i}
is an explicit Gaussian random variable and
A_{i}
depends on the initial data and is independent of
Z_{i}
. The error in this approximation turns out to be the same order as that incurred in (
1.33
) and so we make this approximation before applying the Fourier duality. As
(Z_{i},Z_{j})
are jointly Gaussian and weakly correlated, we find the desired decay. This part of the argument appears in Proposition
4.5
below.

Regime
(1)

We now discuss the regime
(1)
. In this case, we use the representation on the right side of the first line of (
1.2
). Let us first discuss what is expected. Based on the form of the covariance between two resolvents centered at
x
and
y
for large
\eta
, we expect that the estimate,

(1.39)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}G(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)],\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}G(y+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\right)\right|\leq\frac{C}{\eta^{2}+(x-y)^{2}},

holds.
Note that this bound (or a weaker one losing some small polynomial in
N
factors) would imply the desired result as by using a simple Schwarz inequality
|g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)|\leq\frac{|g_{k}(x)|^{2}+|g_{k}(y)|^{2}}{2}
one easily obtains the desired estimate of
C\eta^{-1}\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
for
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))
.

Let us discuss possible approaches to obtaining the estimate (
1.39
). First, in the regime
2^{k}\leq N^{1-\varepsilon}
, or
\eta\geq N^{\varepsilon-1}
, resolvent expansions are stable and in principle the estimate could be obtained in this fashion. Indeed, the paper of Knowles and He
[
38
]
obtains an expansion for the covariance kernel for
x
and
y
in the bulk. However, this still leaves aside the regime
2^{k}\approx N
. The method we use in this regime turns out to easily apply to the entire regime
N^{\varepsilon}\leq 2^{k}\leq N
and so we proceed in this fashion.

Our method is to use a modification of the estimate (
1.33
) in which the family of mesoscopic linear spectral statistics centered at
\gamma_{i}
for each
i
is replaced by two statistics, one centered at
x
and one centered at
y
. This estimate is applied to the second line of the representation (
1.2
). This modified estimate has an additional error term on the right side of (
1.33
) adapted to the distance
|x-\gamma_{i}|
. Since the function
\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}(G(x+\mathrm{i}\eta))]
is sharply peaked at
x
, this error turns out to be acceptable. Here, we choose one
t
for all
x
and
y
which is taken to be large, almost equal to
1
. This part of the argument appears as Proposition
5.3
below.

In the regime under consideration, decay from the Fourier transform of the
\Phi_{t}
cannot help us, as the fluctuations of
\Phi_{t}
are essentially on the scale
\eta
, and so they instead must be computed directly. As specified above, the hope is that, if we can show that the fluctuations of
\Phi_{t}
are universal, then the covariance kernel can be compared to that of the Gaussian ensembles. There are numerous obstacles to this, as mentioned above.

The first issue is calculating the characteristic function of the (almost-global) mesoscopic linear spectral statistic. Here, we must determine it up to an error of
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
in order for the error to be acceptable for
2^{k}\approx N
. To our knowledge, such an estimate does not exist in the literature. In fact, the naive guess that,

(1.40)

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\xi(\Phi_{t}(W)-\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{t}(W)])}]=\mathrm{e}^{-\xi^{2}V_{W}(f_{t})}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})

where
V_{W}
is the functional (
1
) turns out to be false. It turns out that there is an additional correction to
V_{W}
of order
N^{-1/2}
depending on
s_{3}
and moreover a non-Gaussian (multiplicative) correction to the characteristic function of the form
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\xi^{3}N^{-1/2}s_{3}a}
for some coefficient
a
depending on the test function.

That an estimate as strong as (
1.40
) cannot hold was observed by Bao and He
[
5
]
. In this work they considered global linear spectral statistics and subtracted off random correction terms which allowed for an estimate of
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
. This work does not appear applicable to our setting, as we need to calculate the characteristic function for
\Phi_{t}
itself, without any correction terms. Moreover, they handle only global statistics; ours is almost-global but it is not stated how the estimates of
[
5
]
depend on the scale of the test function. We expect that it would enter in a mild fashion, but nonetheless this work does not appear directly applicable.

In the work
[
44
]
we provided an approach to calculating characteristic functions of linear spectral statistics based on cumulant expansions and Stein’s method. In the present work we return to this method and simply calculate a few further terms that appear in the cumulant/resolvent expansions. We find the desired estimate of order
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
after including more non-universal correction terms. This is the content of Section
10
(which uses methods of Section
6
).

At this point, we must figure out how to handle the non-universal corrections. In fact, one term, the
s_{4}
term in the formula (
1
), is in fact of size
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
, the same scale as one extreme of the range considered in the regime
(1)
.

Here, our main observation is that the non-universal terms can, in some sense, be viewed as an additional random shift to the locations
x
and
y
in the covariance function (
1.39
). We implement this observation by de-convolving the function
g_{k}
with a Gaussian of variance
A_{1}N^{-1}
for some large
A_{1}>0
, and consequently convolving the centering
x
of
\mathrm{Im}[\mathrm{tr}(G(x+\mathrm{i}\eta))]
with this Gaussian. Due to the fact that
2^{k}\leq C_{1}N
, this deconvolution action is bounded on
g_{k}
in the
L^{2}
sense. Here, we see the necessity of the restriction of the upper end of the range
(1)
. It is possible that this could be extended to something like
2^{k}\leq c_{1}\sqrt{\log(N)}N
for some
c_{1}>0
adapted to the regularity scale
\mathfrak{a}>0
but this is not necessary for us.

The convolution by a Gaussian of the centering
x
in the covariance function allows us to absorb the non-universal corrections. Essentially, this shows that the covariance of two resolvents for a Gaussian divisible ensemble can be controlled by the covariance of two resolvents of the GOE/GUE after a random shift is of order
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
. This is essentially the content of Propositions
5.9
and
5.11
below. It follows that if we can show that the estimate (
1.39
) holds for the Gaussian ensembles, we will obtain it also for our matrix
H
, and complete the proof; this is due to the fact that the random shift is of smaller or equal order than
\eta
, so introducing the shift on the RHS of (
1.39
) does not affect the resulting estimate.

To our knowledge the estimate (
1.39
) does not appear in the literature for the Gaussian ensembles down to the scales that we require, even for the GUE. One contribution of our work is to establish such an estimate in both the GUE and GOE cases. We establish estimates for the covariance function using the well-known representations for the correlation functions of the Gaussian ensembles in terms of the Hermite polynomials, see, e.g.,
[
48
]
. Here, a significant simplification is afforded by the fact that we are restricted to the spectral bulk in which the asymptotics for the Hermite polynomials are straightforward. In fact, the GUE case is dispensed with quite quickly by relying on these asymptotics and the Christoffel-Darbox formula.

The GOE case is more involved and must be split into the
N
odd and
N
even cases. Here, the cluster functions are essentially “perturbations” of the GUE kernel. There does not seem to be a unified approach to these additional terms and they must instead be checked on a case-by-case basis. For the GOE case, we in fact do not obtain an estimate as strong as (
1.39
) and obtain on the right side a function of the form
\mathcal{O}(\eta^{-1}(|x-y|+\eta)^{-1})
which turns out to be sufficient for our purposes. We expect that this is significantly suboptimal and that (
1.39
) should hold for the GOE, but we do not need this explicit form and do not attempt to prove it. In our estimates, it turns out that the
N
-odd case is more challenging; here we observe a non-obvious (to us, at least) cancellation between two terms that is only evident after some asymptotic analysis of some integrals of Hermite polynomials. We suspect that this has been observed before in the literature but did not find it after a cursory search. The estimates for the Gaussian ensembles appear in Section
8
.

1.2.1
Further discussion

Some of the above methods are tangentially related to the recent work of Cipolloni, Erdős and Schröder
[
17
]
on the spectral form factor defined as,

(1.41)

S(t):=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})}.

In the work
[
17
]
, they derive asymptotics for the expectation and variance of this quantity in the regimes
t\ll N^{5/11}
and
t\ll N^{5/17}
, respectively, using extensive resolvent expansions. Via Fourier duality, one could relate
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))
to
S(t)
. However, our work is primarily focused on larger frequency regimes corresponding to
t\geq N
and consequently there is no overlap in methods or results with those of
[
17
]
.

1.3
Organization of paper

In Section
2
we collect the results from the random matrix literature that we will use in our work. This includes the local and isotropic semicircle laws, Wegner estimates as in
[
24
]
, the homogenization work of Bourgade
[
9
]
, the Littlewood-Paley theory applied to random matrix theory as in
[
55
]
, as well as some standard results on the Gaussian ensembles. In Section
3
we collect the main technical estimates proven in this work. In Section
4
, we establish the desired estimates on
\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(H))
for the submicroscopic frequences
2^{k}\geq C_{1}N
(i.e., the regime
(2)
as discussed in Section
1.2
). In Section
5
we deal with the frequences in the range
N^{\varepsilon}\leq 2^{k}\leq C_{1}N
, i.e., the regime
(1)
as in Section
1.2
. In Section
6
we establish estimates for the covariance of resolvents for large frequencies including near the edge. In Section
7
we apply the method of characteristics to study the mesoscopic linear spectral statistic that arises in the application of Bourgade’s homogenization result. In Section
8
we apply the exact formulas for the cluster functions of the Gaussian ensembles to derive estimates on the covariance of two resolvents with small imaginary part for the GOE and GUE. Section
9
contains the proof of Theorem
1.4
. In Section
10
we establish fine estimates on the characteristic function of almost-global linear spectral statistics via resolvent expansions. The appendices collect various auxilliary results, including a few calculations involving the semicircle distribution, as well as some standard Hermite polynomial asymptotics and an asymptotic calculation of some integrals of Hermite polynomials that arise in the
N
-odd GOE case of Section
8
.

1.4
On the symmetry class of the ensemble

Throughout this paper we will restrict ourselves to the real symmetric Wigner matrices. We will require a few results that are based on the exact calculations for the Gaussian ensembles using the formulas for the correlation functions or cluster functions, which are different for the GOE and GUE. Where we cite results from the literature, we state them for both the GOE/GUE. Our primary use of exact formulas is in our estimates for the covariance of two resolvents. There, we give the proofs for both ensembles.

We state and prove results on the characteristic functions of almost-global linear statistics only in the real symmetric class. The complex Hermitian case can be treated by the exact same method and requires only additional notation, as it based only on resolvent expansions.

For the rest of the paper, the symmetry class of the matrix ensemble under consideration does not make a significant difference to the methods.

1.5
Acknowledgements

The work of Philippe Sosoe is partially supported by NSF grant DMS-1811093.

2
Background

In this section we collect the various results of random matrix theory that we will use in our proofs. This section also serves to collect the various notation that we use.

We will use the following notion of high-probability events.

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a family of events
\mathcal{A}_{i}
indexed by
i\in\mathcal{I}
some index set hold with overwhelming probability if for all
D>0
there is a
C>0
so that

(2.1)

\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}^{c}_{i}]\leq CN^{-D},

for all
N
.

If
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
are two positive quantities indexed by some set
\mathcal{I}
(for example,
N
-dependent sequences, or positive functions on the upper half-plane) then the notation

(2.2)

a_{i}\asymp b_{i}

means that there is a
C>0
so that

(2.3)

\frac{1}{C}a_{i}\leq b_{i}\leq Ca_{i},

for all
i
.

2.1
Local semicircle laws and rigidity

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{N}
. We introduce the following notation,

(2.4)

m_{N}(z):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}-z}

and

(2.5)

m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z):=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{-z+\sqrt{z^{2}-4}}{2}

where Wigner’s semicircle density is given by

(2.6)

\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x):=\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2\pi}\bm{1}_{\{|x|\leq 2\}}.

Note that,

(2.7)

m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)^{2}+zm_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)+1=0.

We will denote the resolvent by,

(2.8)

G_{ij}(z)=\left(\frac{1}{H-z}\right)_{ij}

For any
\tau>0
define,

(2.9)

\mathcal{D}_{\tau,1}:=\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta:|E|\leq\tau,N^{\tau-1}<\eta\leq\tau^{-1}\}.

and

(2.10)

\mathcal{D}_{\tau,2}:=\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta:|E|\leq\tau,0<\eta\leq\tau^{-1}\}.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix. Let
\tau>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, with overwhelming probabiltity we have for all
z\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau,2}
that,

(2.11)

|m_{N}(z)-m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{N\mathrm{Im}[z]}

as well as,

(2.12)

|G_{ij}(z)-\delta_{ij}(z)|\leq N^{\varepsilon}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)]}{N\mathrm{Im}[z]}}+\frac{1}{N\mathrm{Im}[z]}\right)

for all
i,j
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix with resolvent
G(z)
. Let
\tau>0
,
\varepsilon>0
and
D>0
. There is a
C>0
so that,

(2.13)

\mathbb{P}\left[\exists z\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau,1}:\left|\mathbf{x}^{T}G(z)\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{y}m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)\right|>N^{\varepsilon}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)]}{N\mathrm{Im}[z]}}+\frac{1}{N\mathrm{Im}[z]}\right)\right]\leq CN^{-D}

for all unit vectors
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

From the above results, we see that it will be useful to introduce the control parameter,

(2.14)

\Psi(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)]}{N\mathrm{Im}[z]}}+\frac{1}{N|\mathrm{Im}[z]|}.

We will denote the quantiles of the semicircle distribution by
\gamma_{i}
. They are defined by,

(2.15)

\frac{i}{N}=\int_{-2}^{\gamma_{i}}\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x,

where
\gamma_{N}=2
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{N}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
. With overwhelming probability,

(2.16)

|\lambda_{i}-\gamma_{i}|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{N^{2/3}\min\{i^{1/3},(N+1-i)^{1/3}\}},

for every
i
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
we define the distance to the spectral edge as,

(2.17)

\kappa(z):=||\mathrm{Re}[z]|-2||.

For the Stieltjes transform of the semicircle distribution we have the following, see for example
[
7
]
,
[
28
]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
, for
\eta>0
. If
|E|<2
then,

(2.18)

\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)]\asymp\sqrt{\kappa(z)+\eta}

and if
|E|>2
then,

(2.19)

\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)]\asymp\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa(z)+\eta}}.

2.2
Homogenization of Dyson Brownian motion

A Gaussian divisible ensemble is a Wigner matrix
H
such that
H
can be written as independent sum of

(2.20)

H=\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}W+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}}G

where
t>0
,
W
is another real symmetric Wigner matrix and
G
is from the GOE. Note that
H
satisfies (
1.19
) iff
W
does.

Dyson Brownian motion is the solution
\{x_{i}(t)\}_{1\leq i\leq N,t\geq 0}
to the following system of stochastic differential equations,

(2.21)

\mathrm{d}x_{i}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{N}}\mathrm{d}B_{i}(t)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t)}\mathrm{d}t-\frac{x_{i}(t)}{2}\mathrm{d}t,

where
\{B_{i}(t)\}_{i}
is an iid family of standard Brownian motions. If the initial data
\{x_{i}(0)\}_{i=1}^{N}
are distributed as the eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix
H_{0}
with real entries then
x_{i}(t)
has the same distribution as the eigenvalues of the Gaussian divisible ensemble

(2.22)

H_{t}:=\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}H_{0}+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}}G

where
G
is a GOE matrix independent from
H_{0}
.

Associated with DBM are the characteristics:

\displaystyle z_{t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{t/2}(z+\sqrt{z^{2}-4})+\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}(z-\sqrt{z^{2}-4})}{2}

(2.23)

\displaystyle=\cosh(t/2)z+\sinh(t/2)\sqrt{z^{2}-4}.

A straightforward calculation shows that

(2.24)

\partial_{t}z_{t}=m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})+\frac{z_{t}}{2}.

The characteristic is well defined for
\mathrm{Im}[z]>0
. For
E\in\mathbb{R}
we define,

(2.25)

E_{t}=\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}(z+\mathrm{i}\eta)_{t}.

Consider now two DBM process
\{\mu_{i}(t)\}_{i,t}
and
\{\lambda_{i}(t)\}_{i,t}
evolving according to (
2.21
), with the same Brownian motion terms. For the initial data, we assume that
\lambda_{i}(0)
are the eigenvalues of a Wigner matrix
W
and the
\mu_{i}(0)
are the eigenvalues of a Gaussian ensemble of the same symmetry class as
W
. Define,

(2.26)

X^{W}(z,t):=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\sum_{k}\log(\lambda_{k}(0)-z_{t})}{\mathrm{Im}m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})}-N\frac{\int\mathrm{Im}\log(x-z_{t})\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{Im}m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})}

and

(2.27)

X^{G}(z,t):=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\sum_{k}\log(\mu_{k}(0)-z_{t})}{\mathrm{Im}m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})}-N\frac{\int\mathrm{Im}\log(x-z_{t})\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{Im}m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})}

The following Theorem is a reformulation of Theorem 3.1 of
[
9
]
. The exact statement first appeared in
[
12
]
(but see also
[
11
]
for a similar formulation where a solution to a discrete parabolic equation coming from homogenization for DBM was rewritten in terms of a mesoscopic linear spectral statistic). It follows in a straightforward manner from Theorem 3.1 of
[
9
]
as well as the proof of Lemma 3.4 that appears there. This reformulation is useful as the functions
X^{W}
and
X^{G}
are mesoscopic linear spectral statistics which may be studied via resolvent and dynamical methods.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\{\mu_{i}(t)\}_{i}
and
\{\lambda_{i}(t)\}_{i}
be as above. Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta>0
and
\kappa>0
. For any
D>0
there exists a constant
C>0
so that for any
k\in\ldbrack 1,N\rdbrack
and
E
such that
E,\gamma_{k}\in(-2+\kappa,2-\kappa)
we have for any
N^{\delta-1}<t<1
that

(2.28)

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\lambda_{k}(t)-\mu_{k}(t)-\frac{X^{W}(E,t)-X^{G}(E,t)}{N}\right|>N^{\varepsilon}\frac{N|E-\gamma_{k}|+1}{N^{2}t}\right]\leq CN^{-D}.

Proof.
Theorem 3.1 of
[
9
]
gives an estimate for
\lambda_{k}(t)-\mu_{k}(t)
in terms of a function
\bar{u}_{k}(t)
. We then use (3.3) of
[
9
]
to replace it by
(N\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})])^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{Im}[f_{0}(z_{t})]\mathrm{d}\nu
where
f_{0}(z_{t})
is defined in (1.11) of
[
9
]
and contains a parameter
\nu
omitted from the notation. The statement of Lemma 3.4 of
[
9
]
contains a restriction
\eta\gg N^{-1}
in
z_{t}=(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)_{t}
. However one can see from the proof that in our case
s=t
the estimate nonetheless holds with
|\eta+t-s|=0
. Now, by the definition of
f_{0}
one can see that the integral over
\nu
equals our
X^{W}-X^{G}
. ∎

2.3
Littlewood-Paley theory

In this section we review elements of the Littlewood-Paley theory. This was first applied to random matrix theory in the work
[
55
]
, and our treatment is basically identical to the one given there. We will denote the Fourier transform of
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
by

(2.29)

\hat{f}(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\xi x}f(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Given
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
where
\mathcal{S}
is the space of Schwarz distributions, the inhomogeneous Littlewood-Paley decomposition is the sum,

(2.30)

\varphi=\sum_{k\geq-1}\varphi_{k}

where,

(2.31)

\varphi_{-1}=h\star\varphi

and

(2.32)

\varphi_{k}=2^{k}\omega(2^{k}\cdot)\star\varphi,\qquad k\geq 0

where
h
and
\omega
are smooth functions satisfying the following. The Fourier transforms
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\omega}
are even, smooth and of compact support. The function
\hat{h}
is supported in the ball
\{|\xi|\leq 4/3\}
and the function
\hat{\omega}
is supported in the annulus
\mathcal{C}:=\{3/4\leq|\xi|\leq 8/3\}
. Moreover,
0\leq\hat{h},\hat{\omega}\leq 1
and,

(2.33)

\hat{h}(\xi)+\sum_{k\geq 0}\hat{\omega}(2^{-k}\xi)=1.

We have the following, see, e.g.,
[
3
]
.

Theorem 2.7
.

For any
s>0
there is a
C>0
so that,

(2.34)

\frac{1}{C}\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\leq\sum_{k}2^{2ks}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\|\varphi\|^{2}_{H^{s}}

and for any
0<\alpha<1
there is a
C>0
so that,

(2.35)

\frac{1}{C}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq\sup_{k}2^{\alpha k}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{\alpha}}

Following
[
55
]
, the Littlewood-Paley decomposition allows for a convenient decomposition of
\varphi
into convolutions with Poisson kernels at different scales. Define,

(2.36)

P_{\eta}(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\eta}{\eta^{2}+x^{2}}.

Recall,

(2.37)

\hat{P}_{\eta}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\eta|\xi|}.

Define now,

(2.38)

\hat{g}_{k}(\xi)=\mathrm{e}^{2^{-k}|\xi|}\hat{\varphi}_{k}(\xi).

Then, the following is Theorem 5 of
[
55
]
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
s\geq 0
and
0<\beta<\alpha<1
. If
\varphi\in H^{s}
or
\varphi\in C^{\alpha}
, then
\varphi
has a representation,

(2.39)

\varphi(x)=\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}(P_{2^{-k}}\star g_{k})(x)

where the sum on the right is convergent in
H^{s}
if
\varphi\in H^{s}
and in
C^{\beta}
if
\varphi\in C^{\alpha}
. There are constants
C_{s}
and
C_{\alpha,\beta}
such that,

(2.40)

\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}2^{2ks}\|g_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{s}\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}}^{2}

and

(2.41)

\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}2^{\beta k}\|g_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\alpha}}.

In general we will assume that
\varphi
is of compact support. However, the various
g_{k}
and
\varphi_{k}
will not be of compact support. The following controls their decay.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\varphi
be in
H^{s}
or
C^{\alpha}
and be supported in
(-2+\kappa,2-\kappa)
. Let
|x|>2-\kappa
. Then, for every
M>0
there is a
C_{M}>0
so that for any
k\geq 0
,

(2.42)

|\varphi_{k}(x)|\leq C_{M}\frac{2^{k}}{1+2^{kM}||x|-(2-\kappa)|^{M}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}.

The same estimate holds for
g_{k}(x)
.

Proof.
We prove the estimate for
g_{k}
, the estimate for
\varphi_{k}
being simpler. Let
\psi(x)
be defined by,

(2.43)

\hat{\psi}(\xi):=\hat{\omega}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{|\xi|}.

Since
\hat{\omega}
vanishes near the origin, this is a smooth function of compact support. Therefore, for any
M>0
there is a
C_{M}>0
such that,

(2.44)

|\psi(x)|\leq\frac{C_{M}}{(1+|x|)^{M}}.

On the other hand,

(2.45)

g_{k}(x)=2^{k}\int\psi(2^{k}(x-y))\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}y.

For
y\in(-2+\kappa,2-\kappa)
and
|x|>2-\kappa
we have,

(2.46)

|\psi(2^{k}(x-y))|\leq\frac{C_{M}}{1+2^{Mk}||x|-(2-\kappa)|^{M}}

and the claim follows using
\|\varphi\|_{L^{1}}\leq 2\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}
since
\varphi
is of compact support. ∎

Let
\chi
be a smooth even bump function such that
\chi(x)=1
for
|x|\leq 1
and
\chi(x)=0
for
|x|>2
. We have the following.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\varphi\in H^{s}
or
C^{\alpha}
. Let
\lambda\geq 1
and define
f
by

(2.47)

\hat{f}(\xi)=\chi(\lambda^{-1}\xi)\hat{\varphi}(\xi).

Then for any
k\geq 1
there is a
C_{k}>0
such that,

(2.48)

\|f^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{k}\lambda^{k+1/2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}.

Moreover,

(2.49)

\|f\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{H^{s}},\qquad\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{C^{\alpha}}

Proof.
From Lemma 2.1 of
[
3
]
we see,

(2.50)

\|f^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{k}\lambda^{k+1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}}

Note that if
\psi(x)
is the inverse Fourier transform of
\chi
then

(2.51)

f=(\lambda\psi(\lambda\cdot))\star\varphi,

and so the first estimate follows from Young’s inequality for convolutions. The last inequality of the Proposition follows also from Young’s inequality, as well as the fact that if
f_{k}
is the
k
th Littlewood-Paley projection for
f
then,

(2.52)

f_{k}=(\lambda\psi(\lambda\cdot))\star\varphi_{k}.

This completes the proof. ∎

2.4
Wegner-type estimates

We recall the following from
[
25
]
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
H
be a smooth Wigner matrix. Let
\kappa>0
. Let
1/N>\eta>0
. There exists a constant
C>0
so that for all
N\geq 10
we have,

(2.53)

\mathbb{P}\left[\exists i:|\lambda_{i}-E|<\eta\right]\leq CN\eta

for all
E\in(-2+\kappa,2-\kappa)
.

Remark.
This is stated as Theorem 3.4 of
[
25
]
. There, the estimate is given for complex Hermitian Wigner matrices, but as stated in the introduction of the paper, the method and estimate above applies equally well to the real symmetric case.

Note that we do not require such a sharp estimate, i.e., we could allow a weaker estimate with an
N^{\varepsilon}
factor on the RHS. The primary role in the sub-Gaussian assumption (
1.21
) is so that
[
25
]
can be directly cited; in that paper, the primary role of this role is so that the above estimate is obtained with no
N^{\varepsilon}
or
\log(N)^{C}
factors. We do not need such a tight estimate for our methods. ∎

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
H
be a smooth Wigner matrix and let
A>0,\kappa>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a
C>0
so that

(2.54)

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\right]\leq CN^{\varepsilon}.

holds for all
E\in\mathbb{R}
and
\eta\geq N^{-A}
and
\kappa N\leq i\leq(1-\kappa)N
.

Proof.
If
|E-\gamma_{i}|>N^{\varepsilon-1}
then the result holds by rigidity. If
\eta\geq N^{-1}
then the result is trivial. So we may assume that
E\in(-2+\kappa^{\prime},2-\kappa^{\prime})
for some
\kappa^{\prime}>0
and
\eta\leq N^{-1}
. Then, for

(2.55)

K:=\lceil(2\varepsilon-1)\log_{2}(N)-\log_{2}(\eta)\rceil

we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\bm{1}_{\{|\lambda_{i}-E|<\eta\}}\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\bm{1}_{\{2^{k-1}\eta<|\lambda_{i}-E|<2^{k}\eta\}}\right]

(2.56)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\bm{1}_{\{|\lambda_{i}-E|>2^{K}\eta\}}\right].

By the choice of
K
and rigidity we have that the last line is
\mathcal{O}(1)
as,

(2.57)

\mathbb{P}\left[|\lambda_{i}-E|>2^{K}\eta\right]\leq N^{-D}

for any
D>0
. For the first two terms on the RHS we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\bm{1}_{\{|\lambda_{i}-E|<\eta\}}\right]+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\bm{1}_{\{2^{k-1}\eta<|\lambda_{i}-E|<2^{k}\eta\}}\right]

(2.58)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{1}{\eta 2^{k}}\mathbb{P}\left[\exists i:|\lambda_{i}-E|<2^{k}\eta\right]\leq CK,

where the last line follows from Theorem
2.11
. This yields the claim. ∎

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
H
be a smooth Wigner matrix. Let
A>0
,
\kappa>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a
C>0
so that,

(2.59)

\sup_{E\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{\kappa N\leq i\leq(1-\kappa)N}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\right]\leq CN^{\varepsilon}.

Proof.
For any
E
, there are at most
CN^{\varepsilon}
indices such that
|\gamma_{i}-E|\leq N^{\varepsilon-1}
. The contribution from these indices is estimated via Proposition
2.12
. The contribution from the remaining indices is estimated via rigidity and is bounded above by,

(2.60)

\sum_{\kappa N\leq i\leq(1-\kappa)N}\bm{1}_{\{|\gamma_{i}-E|>N^{\varepsilon-1}\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N|\lambda_{i}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta|}\right]\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{i}\leq C\log(N)

and the claim follows. ∎

2.5
Density of states of Gaussian ensembles

The following results on the asymptotic density of states in the bulk for the Gaussian ensembles can be found in
[
31
,
30
]
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
\kappa>0
. Uniformly for
E\in(-2+\kappa,2-\kappa)
, the density of states of the GUE satisfies,

(2.61)

\rho(E)=\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(E)+\frac{(-1)^{N}}{4\pi^{3}N\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(E)^{2}}\cos\left[N\left(\frac{E}{2}\sqrt{4-E^{2}}+2\arcsin\frac{E}{2}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(N^{-2})

and the density of states of the GOE satisfies,

(2.62)

\rho(E)=\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(E)-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}N\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(E)}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-2}).

In Section
8.5
we derive the following which is useful at the edge.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists
C>0
so that, for the GOE and GUE we have,

(2.63)

\left|\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{tr}(\varphi(H))]-N\int\varphi(x)\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)-\left(1-\frac{2}{\beta}\right)\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{\varphi(x)}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq CN^{\varepsilon}\|\varphi\|_{C^{5}}.

where
\beta=1
in the GOE case and
\beta=2
for GUE.

3
Main technical estimates

In this section, we collect the various technical estimates that we prove throughout the paper.

We have the following asymptotics of the covariance of the imaginary part of the empirical Stieltjes transform which is useful on scales
\gg N^{-1/2}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix and
\varepsilon>0
. For any
z=x\pm\mathrm{i}y
and
w=u+\pm\mathrm{i}v
with
u,v\geq N^{-1/2}
we have,

\displaystyle\left|N^{2}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(z)],\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(w)]\right)\right|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\kappa(x)+y}\sqrt{\kappa(u)+v}}\frac{1}{(x-u)^{2}+y^{2}+v^{2}}

(3.1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\kappa(x)+y}+\sqrt{\kappa(u)+v}}\left(\frac{1}{Ny^{2}v}+\frac{1}{Nv^{2}y}\right).

This theorem is proven in Section
6.2
.
Introduce the norm,

(3.2)

\|\varphi\|_{1,w}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\varphi(x)||4-x^{2}|^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x.

For a Wigner matrix
H
with third and fourth cumulants
s_{3}
and
s_{4}
, we introduce a number of functionals which will be used to describe the fluctuations of linear statistics. Let

\displaystyle V(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{-2}^{2}\int_{-2}^{2}\frac{(\varphi(x)-\varphi(u))^{2}}{(x-u)^{2}}\frac{4-xu}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\sqrt{4-u^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle+\frac{s_{4}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(\int_{-2}^{2}\varphi(x)\frac{2-x^{2}}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{2}

(3.3)

\displaystyle-\frac{2s_{3}}{\pi^{2}N^{1/2}}\left(\int_{-2}^{2}\varphi(x)\frac{2-x^{2}}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\right)\left(\int_{-2}^{2}\varphi(x)\frac{x}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\right)

and

(3.4)

\displaystyle B(f)=-\frac{1}{12\pi^{3}}\left(\int_{-2}^{2}f(x)\frac{x}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\right)^{3},

and

(3.5)

\displaystyle e(f)

\displaystyle=e_{1}(f)+e_{2}(f)

where

(3.6)

\displaystyle e_{1}(f)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int f(x)\frac{1}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{f(2)+f(-2)}{4}

and

(3.7)

\displaystyle e_{2}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{4}}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}f(x)\frac{x^{4}-4x^{2}+2}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{2s_{3}}{N^{1/2}}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}f(x)\frac{3x-x^{3}}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}

The same resolvent expansions used to prove the above result will yield the following.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix and let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be given, and assume
\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{1,w}\leq N
and that the support of
\varphi
is in
[-5,5]
. For all
\varepsilon>0
there is a
C>0
so that,

(3.8)

\left|\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H))-V(\varphi)\right|\leq CN^{-1+\varepsilon}\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{1,w},

and

(3.9)

\left|\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H)]-N\int\varphi(x)\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x-e(\varphi)\right|\leq CN^{-1+\varepsilon}\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{1,w},

and for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
we have,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\xi(\mathrm{tr}\varphi(H)-N\int\varphi(x)\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)-e(\varphi))\right)\right]-\exp\left(-\xi^{2}V(f)/2+\mathrm{i}\xi^{3}s_{3}N^{-1/2}B(f)\right)\right|

(3.10)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CN^{\varepsilon}(1+|\xi|^{6}+\|\varphi^{\prime}\|_{1,w}^{7})N^{-1}\|\varphi^{\prime\prime}\|_{1,w}

Remark.
If
\varphi
is not of compact support, then the above estimates still hold with additional error terms. Let
A_{1}:=\|\varphi|_{[-4,4]^{c}}\|_{\infty}
. For any
D>0
, the variance estimate holds with an additional error of order
(1+A_{1})^{2}N^{-D}
, the expectation estimate with an additional error of order
A_{1}N^{-D}
and the estimate for the characteristic function with an additional error of order
(1+|\xi|)(1+A_{1})N^{-D}
. ∎

Theorem
3.2
is proven in Section
10.2
.
Consider now
\varphi
and its Littlewood-Paley decomposition,

(3.11)

\varphi=\sum_{k}\varphi_{k}.

We will derive estimates for
k
in various ranges.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix of the form,

(3.12)

H=\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}W+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-T}}G

where
G
is an independent GOE matrix. Assume that,

(3.13)

T=N^{-\omega},\qquad 0<\omega<\frac{1}{100}.

Fix a small constant
\mathfrak{b}
satisfying,

(3.14)

0<\mathfrak{b}<\frac{1}{100}.

Let
k
satisfy,

(3.15)

\mathfrak{b}\log_{2}(N)\leq k\leq\log_{2}(N\mathfrak{b}^{-1}).

Then for all
\varepsilon>0
there is a
C>0
(depending also on
\mathfrak{b}
and
\omega>0
) so that,

(3.16)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(H))\leq CN^{\varepsilon}T^{-1}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}2^{k}+CN^{-100}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}

This theorem is proven at the end of Section
5
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
T=N^{-\omega}
, for
0<\omega<\frac{1}{10}
. Let
H
be a Wigner matrix of the form

(3.17)

H=\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}W+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-T}}G

where
G
is an independent Gaussian matrix and
W
is a smooth Wigner matrix. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\delta>\omega
. Let
A>0
and
M>0
. There is a
B_{1}>0
depending on
\delta-\omega
and a
C>0
depending on all of these parameters so that for
k
satisfying,

(3.18)

\log_{2}(B_{1}N)\leq k\leq 2\log_{2}(N)

we have,

(3.19)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))\leq C\left(N^{\delta}N^{\varepsilon}2^{k}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+2^{-Mk}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\right)

Moreover, for
k
satisfying

(3.20)

2\log_{2}(N)\leq k\leq A\log_{2}(N)

we have for any smooth Wigner matrix,

(3.21)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))\leq C\left(N^{\varepsilon}2^{k}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+2^{-Mk}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\right)

for any
\varepsilon>0
.

The above theorem is proven in Section
4.1
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\varphi\in H^{1/2+s}
for some
s>0
. Let
s>\varepsilon>0
. There is a
C>0
so that for any
M\geq 10^{4}
we have,

(3.22)

\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_{k\geq M}\mathrm{tr}\varphi_{k}(H)\right)\leq CN^{2}2^{M(\varepsilon-2s)}\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2+s}}^{2}

Proof.
Let
0<\rho<s
. If
\varphi
is a function whose Fourier transform is supported only on frequencies larger than
100\times 2^{L}
for
L\geq 10
, then for
\rho>s
we have,

(3.23)

\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2+\rho}}^{2}\leq C\sum_{k=L}^{\infty}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}2^{2k(1/2+\rho)}\leq C2^{2L(\rho-s)}\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2+s}}^{2}.

By the Sobolev embedding theorem (e.g., Theorem 1.66 of
[
3
]
) we have,

(3.24)

\|f\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\rho}\|f\|_{H^{1/2+\rho}},

for any
\rho>0
.
We have the trivial inequality,

(3.25)

\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}f(H))\leq N^{2}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}.

The claim follows by taking
L=M-100
. ∎

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
G
be a GOE or GUE matrix and let
\kappa>0
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be small and
\mathfrak{c}>0
be large. There is a constant
C>0
so that for all
|x|,|y|<2-\kappa
, and
1>\eta>N^{-\mathfrak{c}}

(3.26)

N^{2}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)],\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(y+\mathrm{i}\eta)]\right)\leq CN^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{1}{\eta(|x-y|+\eta)}\right).

Proof.
This follows from Propositions
8.1
,
8.4
and
8.13
. ∎

4
Sub-microscopic frequencies

Let
H
be a Wigner matrix.
Let
\mathfrak{s}>0
. We consider
\varphi\in H^{1/2+\mathfrak{s}}
or
C^{1/2+\mathfrak{s}}
. Recall the definition of
\varphi_{k}
and
g_{k}
as in Section
2.3
. In this section we choose a large
B_{1}>0
and
A>0
and consider
k
in the range,

(4.1)

\log_{2}(B_{1}N)\leq k\leq A\log_{2}(N).

We will assume
B_{1}\geq 1
for definiteness. Constants in this section will be allowed to depend on
A
and
B_{1}
, but the dependence on
B_{1}
will be stated explicitly, as its role will become apparent only later in the section.

We consider the representation

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\mathrm{tr}(\varphi_{k}(H)))

(4.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\int g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\,\mathrm{Cov}(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}])\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

where, throughout this section,

(4.3)

\eta:=2^{-k}.

Due to Proposition
2.9
we will see that it will suffice to consider pairs such that both
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
are in the spectral bulk. To facilitate this, introduce the notation,

(4.4)

J_{\alpha}:=[\![\alpha N,(1-\alpha)N]\!],

for an
\alpha>0
.
Fixing a
\delta_{1}>0
we treat separately the contribution from indices
i,j
such that
|i-j|\leq N^{\delta_{1}}/(N\eta)
or otherwise. The contribution from the indices that are close together is estimated in the following Proposition.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
H
be a smooth Wigner matrix, and let
\delta_{1}>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, for

(4.5)

\log_{2}(N)\leq k\leq A\log_{2}(N)

we have,

\displaystyle\sum_{(i,j)\in J_{\alpha}^{2}}\bm{1}_{\{|i-j|<\frac{N^{\delta_{1}}}{N\eta}\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)|\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(4.6)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{N^{\delta_{1}+\varepsilon}}{\eta}\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{(i,j)\in J_{\alpha}^{2}}\bm{1}_{\{|i-j|<\frac{N^{\delta_{1}}}{N\eta}\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)|\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\big]\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(4.7)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{N^{\delta_{1}+\varepsilon}}{\eta}\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}

Proof.
We prove only the first estimate, the second being similar. By estimating
|g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)|\leq|g_{k}(x)|^{2}+|g_{k}(y)|^{2}
and symmetry it suffices to estimate,

\displaystyle\sum_{(i,j)\in J_{\alpha}^{2}}\bm{1}_{\{|i-j|<\frac{N^{\delta_{1}}}{N\eta}\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|g_{k}(x)|^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\big]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\sum_{(i,j)\in J_{\alpha}^{2}}\bm{1}_{\{|i-j|<\frac{N^{\delta_{1}}}{N\eta}\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g_{k}(x)|^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\big]\mathrm{d}x

(4.8)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{N^{\delta_{1}}}{N\eta}\sum_{i\in J_{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g_{k}(x)|^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\big]\mathrm{d}x.

In the first inequality we used the fact that,

(4.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\eta}{(s-y)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\leq C

for any
s
, and in the second inequality we did the sum over
j
, using that the quantity no longer depends on
j
. Now by Proposition
2.13
we have,

(4.10)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in J_{\alpha}}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]]\leq CN^{\varepsilon},

for all
x
. The estimate with the norm
\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}
follows after integration over the
x
variable.∎

The following deals with the contribution from the edge.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
M>0
.
Assume
\varphi
is supported in
[-2+\kappa,2-\kappa]
and let
k
satisfy,

(4.11)

\log_{2}(N)\leq k\leq A\log_{2}(N).

Assume that
\alpha>0
is so small that
\gamma_{\alpha N}<-2+\kappa/4
. Then,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{(i,j)\notin J_{\alpha}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\right|

(4.12)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CN^{\varepsilon}\left(\eta^{-1}\|g_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+2^{-Mk}N^{2}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Proof.
By Proposition
2.9
it suffices to consider the integral over
D:=[-2+\kappa/2,2-\kappa/2]^{2}
. Clearly,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{(i,j)\notin J_{\alpha}^{2}}\int_{D}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left|\int_{D}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}\left(N\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)],\sum_{j\notin J_{\alpha}}\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\right|

(4.13)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 4\int_{D}|g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)|\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_{i\notin J_{\alpha}}\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

By the rigidity estimates and the fact that if
(x,y)\in D
, then
x
is separated from
\gamma_{i}
for
i\notin J_{\alpha}
by a constant independent of
N
, we easily see that,

(4.14)

\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_{i\notin J_{\alpha}}\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right)\leq N^{\varepsilon}

for any
\varepsilon>0
and
N
large enough. By the local law,
\mathrm{Var}(N\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)])\leq N^{\varepsilon}/\eta^{2}
. Therefore by Cauchy-Schwarz,

(4.15)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(N\mathrm{Im}[m_{N}(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)],\sum_{j\notin J_{\alpha}}\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\right)\right|\leq CN^{\varepsilon}\eta^{-1}

and the claim follows. ∎

In what follows we will focus on pairs of indices
(i,j)\in J_{\alpha}^{2}
.
Fix now
\omega>0
and assume that
H
is a Gaussian divisible ensemble with Gaussian component of size

(4.16)

T=N^{-\omega},

and fix
\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}>\delta_{3}>\omega>0
. For each
i,j
we define

(4.17)

t_{ij}:=N^{-\delta_{2}}|\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}|,\qquad T_{ij}:=N^{-\delta_{3}}|\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}|.

For
i
and
j
satisfying
|i-j|\geq N^{\delta_{1}}(N\eta)^{-1}
note that the error in Theorem
2.6
satisfies,

(4.18)

\frac{1}{N^{2}t_{ij}}\asymp\frac{N^{\delta_{2}}}{N|i-j|}\leq N^{\delta_{2}-\delta_{1}}\eta\ll\eta

under the assumption that
\delta_{1}>\delta_{2}
. Note also that,

(4.19)

t_{ij}=N^{-\delta_{2}}|\gamma_{i}-\gamma_{j}|\asymp\frac{N^{-\delta_{2}}}{N}|i-j|\geq\frac{N^{\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}}}{N}\frac{1}{N\eta}\geq\frac{N^{\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}}}{N}\gg\frac{1}{N}.

This partially motivates the above choices.

The goal of the remainder of the section is to estimate the quantity,

(4.20)

\mathrm{Cov}(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}])

for fixed
i
and
j
. In order to do so we take a somewhat non-trivial coupling of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(t)
to an equilibrium process which we will now outline. In particular, the coupling depends on the pair of indices chosen,
(i,j)
.

Recall that our eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k}
are the eigenvalues of a Gaussian divisible ensemble with Gaussian component of size
T
, which we denote by
H_{T}
, with

(4.21)

H_{t}:=\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}H_{0}+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}}G

where
G
is a Gaussian matrix independent from
H_{0}
, some fixed Wigner matrix.
These have the same distribution as the solution to (
2.21
), which we denote by
\{\lambda_{k}(s)\}_{k}
at time
s=T_{ij}+t_{ij}
with initial data have the same distribution as an independent copy of
H_{T-T_{ij}-t_{ij}}
. Denote the Brownian motions driving (
2.21
) by
B_{k}(s)
.

Consider now an auxilliary process
\mu_{k}(s)
which solves (
2.21
) for times
T_{ij}\leq s\leq T_{ij}+t_{ij}
with the same Brownian terms
B_{k}(s)
(but only for these times
s
), with initial data
\mu_{k}(T_{ij})
being the eigenvalues of an independent Gaussian ensemble.

Clearly, the three families of random variables,

(4.22)

\{\mu_{k}(s)\}_{k,s\geq T_{ij}},\qquad\{\lambda_{k}(0)\}_{k},\qquad\{B_{k}(s)\}_{k,0\leq s\leq T_{ij}}

are mutually independent.

By Theorem
2.6
we have for any
\varepsilon>0
(we will choose also
\varepsilon<\delta_{2}-\delta_{1}
) that,

(4.23)

|\lambda_{i}(T_{ij}+t_{ij})-\mu_{i}(T_{ij}+t_{ij})-N^{-1}(X_{1}(\gamma_{i},t_{ij})-X_{2}(\gamma_{i},t_{ij}))|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{N^{2}t_{ij}},

where

(4.24)

X_{1}(z,t)=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\sum_{n}\log(\lambda_{n}(T_{ij})-z_{t})}{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})]}-N\frac{\int\mathrm{Im}[\log(x-z_{t})]\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})]}

and

(4.25)

X_{2}(z,t)=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\sum_{n}\log(\mu_{n}(T_{ij})-z_{t})}{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})]}-N\frac{\int\mathrm{Im}[\log(x-z_{t})]\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z_{t})]},

and an analogous estimate changing
i
for
j
. By Proposition
7.2
we have for
X_{1}(\gamma_{i},t_{ij})
and
X_{1}(\gamma_{j},t_{ij})
the estimates,

(4.26)

\displaystyle X_{1}(\gamma_{i},t_{ij})=Z_{i}+Y_{i}+N^{\varepsilon}\mathcal{O}((Nt_{ij})^{-1})

for any
\varepsilon>0
with overwhelming probability where

\displaystyle Y_{i}:=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{n}\mathrm{Im}[\log(\lambda_{n}(0)-(\gamma_{i})_{T_{ij}+t_{ij}})]}{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}((\gamma_{i})_{t_{ij}})]}-\frac{N\int\mathrm{Im}\log(x-(\gamma_{i})_{T_{ij}+t_{ij}})\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}((\gamma_{i})_{t_{ij}})]}

(4.27)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{2}{\beta}\right)\int_{0}^{T_{ij}}\mathrm{Im}[m_{\mathrm{sc}}^{\prime}((\gamma_{i})_{t_{ij}+s})]\mathrm{d}s

and

(4.28)

\displaystyle Z_{i}:=\frac{1}{N^{1/2}}\sum_{n}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\int_{0}^{T_{ij}}\mathrm{Im}[(\gamma_{n}-(\gamma_{i})_{T_{ij}+t_{ij}-s})^{-1}]\mathrm{d}B_{n}(s)

and analogous definitions for
Y_{j}
and
Z_{j}
. Above,
\beta=1
in the case of real symmetric Wigner matrices that we consider. It is
\beta=2
for the complex Hermitian case.

Here
(\gamma_{i})_{t_{ij}}
denotes the solution at time
t_{ij}
of the characteristic flow (
2.2
) with initial point
z=\gamma_{i}+\mathrm{i}0
(see also (
2.25
)).

Making the additional definition

(4.29)

\hat{\mu}^{(ij)}_{i}=\mu_{i}(T_{ij}+t_{ij})-N^{-1}X_{2}(\gamma_{i},t_{ij})

we conclude from the above discussion as well as
Proposition
7.5
the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. With overwhelming probability, we have

(4.30)

|\lambda_{i}(T_{ij}+t_{ij})-\hat{\mu}^{(ij)}_{i}-N^{-1}(Y_{i}-Z_{i})|\leq\frac{N^{\varepsilon}}{N^{2}t_{ij}}

and an analogous estimate for
\lambda_{j}
. The three families of random variables
(Z_{i},Z_{j})
,
(Y_{i},Y_{j})
and
(\hat{\mu}^{(ij)}_{i},\hat{\mu}^{(ij)}_{j})
are mutually independent, and the
Z_{i},Z_{j}
are jointly Gaussian. Moreover, there is a
c>0
so that,

(4.31)

c(\delta_{2}-\delta_{3})\log(N)\leq\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i}),\mathrm{Var}(Z_{j})\leq c^{-1}(\delta_{2}-\delta_{3})\log(N)

and,

(4.32)

|\mathrm{Cov}(Z_{i},Z_{j})|\leq\frac{1}{c\log(N)}.

For notational simplicity, define also

(4.33)

\hat{\lambda}^{(ij)}_{i}:=\hat{\mu}^{(ij)}_{i}-N^{-1}(Y_{i}+Z_{i}).

The following is one of the main technical arguments in the paper, as obtaining an acceptable error estimate is delicate.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
k
satisfy

(4.34)

\log_{2}(N)\leq k\leq A\log_{2}(N).

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
M>0
there is a
C>0
so that,

\displaystyle\sum_{(i,j)\in J^{2}_{\alpha}}\bm{1}_{\{|i-j|>\frac{N^{\delta_{1}}}{N\eta}\}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{k}(x)g_{k}(y)\mathrm{Cov}\big(\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\lambda}^{(ij)}_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\lambda}^{(ij)}_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}]\big)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\bigg|

(4.35)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{N^{\varepsilon+\delta_{2}}}{\eta}\|\varphi_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+2^{-Mk}N^{2}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}\right)

Proof.
We first remark that by using Proposition
2.9
we can restrict both of the integrals above to the square

(4.36)

R:=[-5,5]^{2}

at the cost of
C_{M}2^{-Mk}N^{2}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}
, for any
M>0
. In what follows we will only consider the integration over such
x,y
and ignore the remaining contributions.

From the estimate (
4.30
) and the fact that our parameters satisfy (
4.18
) we see that for any
\varepsilon>0
we have,

\displaystyle\bigg|

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\lambda_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\lambda}^{(ij)}_{i}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}],\mathrm{Im}[(\hat{\lambda}^{(ij)}_{j}-y-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1})\bigg|

(4.37)

\displaystyle\leq
